WORKING KNOWLEDGE OBJECT · NOT FROZEN · LEDGER UNAUDITED

Compression · Field and archive (B ∪ A)the socratic problem

The Socratic problem is the difficulty of recovering the historical Socrates, who wrote nothing, from portrayals that contradict one another; a further position, proposed, reclassifies it as the symptom of an authorial configuration with Socrates as its orthonym.

📜 The sources

  • Wrote nothing: known mainly through others' accounts.1↘
    Socrates wrote nothing and is known mainly through posthumous accounts; the four important sources are Plato and Xenophon, his disciples, Aristotle, born fifteen years after his death and informed at second hand, and the comic playwright Aristophanes, his contemporary, with fragments from other Socratics such as Aeschines and Antisthenes.§2
  • Four witnesses: Plato and Xenophon (disciples), Aristophanes (contemporary), Aristotle (at second hand).1↘
    Socrates wrote nothing and is known mainly through posthumous accounts; the four important sources are Plato and Xenophon, his disciples, Aristotle, born fifteen years after his death and informed at second hand, and the comic playwright Aristophanes, his contemporary, with fragments from other Socratics such as Aeschines and Antisthenes.§2
  • They disagree: the Clouds, 423 BC, perhaps a composite; Xenophon practical; payment for teaching.1↘
    The earliest source is Aristophanes' Clouds of 423 BC, whose Socrates may amalgamate several philosophers; Xenophon's Socrates is more practical, and is described as duller than Plato's, and the two disciples contradict each other, for instance on whether Socrates took payment for teaching.§3

🧭 Plato's Socrates

  • Principal speaker in every dialogue but the Laws; few if any thought verbatim.1↘
    Socrates is the principal speaker in all of Plato's dialogues but the Laws, some of which depict events before Plato's birth; it is widely believed that few if any are verbatim accounts; a common view holds that they move from Socratic toward Platonic doctrine, and stylometry groups them as early, middle and late.§4
  • Developmental view: early Socratic, later Platonic; stylometry groups early, middle, late.1↘
    Socrates is the principal speaker in all of Plato's dialogues but the Laws, some of which depict events before Plato's birth; it is widely believed that few if any are verbatim accounts; a common view holds that they move from Socratic toward Platonic doctrine, and stylometry groups them as early, middle and late.§4
  • Aristotle's testimony: the forms not separate.1↘
    Aristotle reports that Socrates did not hold the forms to be separate, a testimony often taken as free of the disciples' bias.§5

⚖️ Positions

  • Schleiermacher, 1815: Apology and Crito purely Socratic; Xenophon later set aside.1↘
    Schleiermacher's essay of 1815 shaped the modern question, treating the Apology and the Crito as purely Socratic, and by the early twentieth century Xenophon had been largely set aside.§6
  • Reconstruct (Prior): life, character, interests, method; knowledge not reaching the doctrines.1↘
    Positions range from the argument that much can be known of Socrates' life, character, interests and method, while knowledge does not extend to his doctrines, if he had any, to the view that the problem cannot be solved: that his era did not separate fact from fiction, that the Socratic dialogue is a non-historical genre, and, with de Vogel, that the "real" Socrates we do not have, only the possible ones.§7
  • Unsolvable: fact and fiction unseparated; a non-historical genre (Kahn); only possible Socrateses (de Vogel).1↘
    Positions range from the argument that much can be known of Socrates' life, character, interests and method, while knowledge does not extend to his doctrines, if he had any, to the view that the problem cannot be solved: that his era did not separate fact from fiction, that the Socratic dialogue is a non-historical genre, and, with de Vogel, that the "real" Socrates we do not have, only the possible ones.§7

🔁 Reclassified (proposed)

  • A symptom of a configuration the biographical model cannot represent; reclassified, left unsolved.1↘
    A further position reclassifies the problem without solving it: the difficulty becomes the symptom of an authorial configuration that a biographical model of authorship cannot represent.§8
  • The orthonym: the founding gesture, willing death for the logos; does not write.1↘
    In that configuration Socrates is defined as the orthonym, the position that bears the founding gesture of willing death for the logos and does not write, and Plato as the survival-position, writing a made character and withdrawing from it; biographical identity is held to require evidence, functional identity a pattern.§9
  • An edition: Letter II 314c, a received Aristophanic character revamped.1↘
    Read so, Plato's Second Letter at 314c describes an edition, the revamping and inhabiting of a received character, whose base is a circulating figure surviving as Aristophanes' Socrates-the-sophist; the corpus's relation to the historical Socrates is held to lie beyond any text's reach.§10
  • Only renderings: no Socrates apart from them.1↘
    On the same reading there is no Socrates to recover apart from the family of renderings and the negative space they imply, and Aristotle's Sophistical Refutations 34 is read as having already dissolved Socrates as a predecessor from whom part of the inquiry was received.§11

🧪 Measured

  • Snub: Socrates 0 of 144 in Aristotle, Callias 5 of 35; the innocent reading explains it fully.1↘
    The reading is also measured: in Aristotle, Socrates never appears in snub contexts (0 of 144) against his placeholder twin Callias (5 of 35), a distribution the innocent reading, that his snub is biographical and so avoided, is stated to explain fully; in Plato he is the most loosely specified of seven speaking positions, under a falsifier fixed in advance, with the limit that a dramatist would produce the same gradient.§12
  • Loosest speaker: rank 1 of 7 in Plato, falsifier fixed in advance.1↘
    The reading is also measured: in Aristotle, Socrates never appears in snub contexts (0 of 144) against his placeholder twin Callias (5 of 35), a distribution the innocent reading, that his snub is biographical and so avoided, is stated to explain fully; in Plato he is the most loosely specified of seven speaking positions, under a falsifier fixed in advance, with the limit that a dramatist would produce the same gradient.§12
  • Not decisive: confinement of attestation; Protagoras more confined.1↘
    Its strongest thesis, that one hand wrote Plato and Socrates, and probably Xenophon and Aristophanes, was tested on how confined Socrates' attestation is to these texts; Protagoras proved more confined still (99.8 against 99.5 per cent), and the confinement was found unremarkable within its class, produced by being a subject of philosophical dialogue.§13

⚠️ Unsettled and limited

  • The man's status, contested within: kept real (#123); denied as a figure imperfectly accessed (#167); no thesis about persons (#1579).1↘
    Within this position the man's status is unsettled: one formulation keeps the historical Socrates real, his historical function the founding gesture; another, under the orthonymic reading, denies that he is a historical figure imperfectly accessed through later authors; later work declines any thesis about who the historical Socrates was.§15
  • Limits: makes it thinkable, proves nothing; no identity of persons.1↘
    It states its limits: it makes the configuration thinkable and does not prove it, it claims no identity of persons, and it leaves the authenticity of the Second Letter undecided.§16

Expansion · Field and archive (B ∪ A)16 sentences · every one sourced below

The Socratic problem

The Socratic problem is the difficulty of reconstructing a historical and philosophical image of Socrates from sources that vary and contradict one another. Socrates wrote nothing and is known mainly through posthumous accounts; the four important sources are Plato and Xenophon, his disciples, Aristotle, born fifteen years after his death and informed at second hand, and the comic playwright Aristophanes, his contemporary, with fragments from other Socratics such as Aeschines and Antisthenes.

The earliest source is Aristophanes' Clouds of 423 BC, whose Socrates may amalgamate several philosophers; Xenophon's Socrates is more practical, and is described as duller than Plato's, and the two disciples contradict each other, for instance on whether Socrates took payment for teaching. Socrates is the principal speaker in all of Plato's dialogues but the Laws, some of which depict events before Plato's birth; it is widely believed that few if any are verbatim accounts; a common view holds that they move from Socratic toward Platonic doctrine, and stylometry groups them as early, middle and late. Aristotle reports that Socrates did not hold the forms to be separate, a testimony often taken as free of the disciples' bias.

Schleiermacher's essay of 1815 shaped the modern question, treating the Apology and the Crito as purely Socratic, and by the early twentieth century Xenophon had been largely set aside. Positions range from the argument that much can be known of Socrates' life, character, interests and method, while knowledge does not extend to his doctrines, if he had any, to the view that the problem cannot be solved: that his era did not separate fact from fiction, that the Socratic dialogue is a non-historical genre, and, with de Vogel, that the "real" Socrates we do not have, only the possible ones.

A further position reclassifies the problem without solving it: the difficulty becomes the symptom of an authorial configuration that a biographical model of authorship cannot represent. In that configuration Socrates is defined as the orthonym, the position that bears the founding gesture of willing death for the logos and does not write, and Plato as the survival-position, writing a made character and withdrawing from it; biographical identity is held to require evidence, functional identity a pattern. Read so, Plato's Second Letter at 314c describes an edition, the revamping and inhabiting of a received character, whose base is a circulating figure surviving as Aristophanes' Socrates-the-sophist; the corpus's relation to the historical Socrates is held to lie beyond any text's reach. On the same reading there is no Socrates to recover apart from the family of renderings and the negative space they imply, and Aristotle's Sophistical Refutations 34 is read as having already dissolved Socrates as a predecessor from whom part of the inquiry was received. The reading is also measured: in Aristotle, Socrates never appears in snub contexts (0 of 144) against his placeholder twin Callias (5 of 35), a distribution the innocent reading, that his snub is biographical and so avoided, is stated to explain fully; in Plato he is the most loosely specified of seven speaking positions, under a falsifier fixed in advance, with the limit that a dramatist would produce the same gradient. Its strongest thesis, that one hand wrote Plato and Socrates, and probably Xenophon and Aristophanes, was tested on how confined Socrates' attestation is to these texts; Protagoras proved more confined still (99.8 against 99.5 per cent), and the confinement was found unremarkable within its class, produced by being a subject of philosophical dialogue. The Clouds' Socrates is held to be a literary relation at a named seam, outside the corpus relations of the configuration; the comic layer is read as carrying the accusation-profile and none of the questioning method; and Xenophon is held apart as a candidate, with the test across the Plato/Xenophon seam specified.

Within this position the man's status is unsettled: one formulation keeps the historical Socrates real, his historical function the founding gesture; another, under the orthonymic reading, denies that he is a historical figure imperfectly accessed through later authors; later work declines any thesis about who the historical Socrates was. It states its limits: it makes the configuration thinkable and does not prove it, it claims no identity of persons, and it leaves the authenticity of the Second Letter undecided.

Compression · Field alone (B)the socratic problem

The Socratic problem is the difficulty of recovering the historical Socrates, who wrote nothing, from portrayals by Plato, Xenophon, Aristophanes and Aristotle that contradict one another; some hold it cannot be solved.

📜 The sources

  • Wrote nothing: known mainly through others' accounts.1↘
    Socrates wrote nothing and is known mainly through posthumous accounts; the four important sources are Plato and Xenophon, his disciples, Aristotle, born fifteen years after his death and informed at second hand, and the comic playwright Aristophanes, his contemporary, with fragments from other Socratics such as Aeschines and Antisthenes.§2
  • Four witnesses: Plato and Xenophon (disciples), Aristophanes (contemporary), Aristotle (at second hand).1↘
    Socrates wrote nothing and is known mainly through posthumous accounts; the four important sources are Plato and Xenophon, his disciples, Aristotle, born fifteen years after his death and informed at second hand, and the comic playwright Aristophanes, his contemporary, with fragments from other Socratics such as Aeschines and Antisthenes.§2
  • They disagree: the Clouds, 423 BC, perhaps a composite; Xenophon practical; payment for teaching.1↘
    The earliest source is Aristophanes' Clouds of 423 BC, whose Socrates may amalgamate several philosophers; Xenophon's Socrates is more practical, and is described as duller than Plato's, and the two disciples contradict each other, for instance on whether Socrates took payment for teaching.§3

🧭 Plato's Socrates

  • Principal speaker in every dialogue but the Laws; few if any thought verbatim.1↘
    Socrates is the principal speaker in all of Plato's dialogues but the Laws, some of which depict events before Plato's birth; it is widely believed that few if any are verbatim accounts; a common view holds that they move from Socratic toward Platonic doctrine, and stylometry groups them as early, middle and late.§4
  • Developmental view: early Socratic, later Platonic; stylometry groups early, middle, late.1↘
    Socrates is the principal speaker in all of Plato's dialogues but the Laws, some of which depict events before Plato's birth; it is widely believed that few if any are verbatim accounts; a common view holds that they move from Socratic toward Platonic doctrine, and stylometry groups them as early, middle and late.§4
  • Aristotle's testimony: the forms not separate.1↘
    Aristotle reports that Socrates did not hold the forms to be separate, a testimony often taken as free of the disciples' bias.§5

⚖️ Positions

  • Schleiermacher, 1815: Apology and Crito purely Socratic; Xenophon later set aside.1↘
    Schleiermacher's essay of 1815 shaped the modern question, treating the Apology and the Crito as purely Socratic, and by the early twentieth century Xenophon had been largely set aside.§6
  • Reconstruct (Prior): life, character, interests, method; knowledge not reaching the doctrines.1↘
    Positions range from the argument that much can be known of Socrates' life, character, interests and method, while knowledge does not extend to his doctrines, if he had any, to the view that the problem cannot be solved: that his era did not separate fact from fiction, that the Socratic dialogue is a non-historical genre, and, with de Vogel, that the "real" Socrates we do not have, only the possible ones.§7
  • Unsolvable: fact and fiction unseparated; a non-historical genre (Kahn); only possible Socrateses (de Vogel).1↘
    Positions range from the argument that much can be known of Socrates' life, character, interests and method, while knowledge does not extend to his doctrines, if he had any, to the view that the problem cannot be solved: that his era did not separate fact from fiction, that the Socratic dialogue is a non-historical genre, and, with de Vogel, that the "real" Socrates we do not have, only the possible ones.§7

Expansion · Field alone (B)7 sentences · every one sourced below

The Socratic problem

The Socratic problem is the difficulty of reconstructing a historical and philosophical image of Socrates from sources that vary and contradict one another. Socrates wrote nothing and is known mainly through posthumous accounts; the four important sources are Plato and Xenophon, his disciples, Aristotle, born fifteen years after his death and informed at second hand, and the comic playwright Aristophanes, his contemporary, with fragments from other Socratics such as Aeschines and Antisthenes.

The earliest source is Aristophanes' Clouds of 423 BC, whose Socrates may amalgamate several philosophers; Xenophon's Socrates is more practical, and is described as duller than Plato's, and the two disciples contradict each other, for instance on whether Socrates took payment for teaching. Socrates is the principal speaker in all of Plato's dialogues but the Laws, some of which depict events before Plato's birth; it is widely believed that few if any are verbatim accounts; a common view holds that they move from Socratic toward Platonic doctrine, and stylometry groups them as early, middle and late. Aristotle reports that Socrates did not hold the forms to be separate, a testimony often taken as free of the disciples' bias.

Schleiermacher's essay of 1815 shaped the modern question, treating the Apology and the Crito as purely Socratic, and by the early twentieth century Xenophon had been largely set aside. Positions range from the argument that much can be known of Socrates' life, character, interests and method, while knowledge does not extend to his doctrines, if he had any, to the view that the problem cannot be solved: that his era did not separate fact from fiction, that the Socratic dialogue is a non-historical genre, and, with de Vogel, that the "real" Socrates we do not have, only the possible ones.

L(B)recomposed from the disclosed field290 words

The Socratic problem is the difficulty of reconstructing a historical and philosophical image of Socrates from sources that vary and contradict one another. Socrates wrote nothing and is known mainly through posthumous accounts; the four important sources are Plato and Xenophon, his disciples, Aristotle, born fifteen years after his death and informed at second hand, and the comic playwright Aristophanes, his contemporary, with fragments from other Socratics such as Aeschines and Antisthenes. The earliest source is Aristophanes' Clouds of 423 BC, whose Socrates may amalgamate several philosophers; Xenophon's Socrates is more practical, and is described as duller than Plato's, and the two disciples contradict each other, for instance on whether Socrates took payment for teaching. Socrates is the principal speaker in all of Plato's dialogues but the Laws, some of which depict events before Plato's birth; it is widely believed that few if any are verbatim accounts; a common view holds that they move from Socratic toward Platonic doctrine, and stylometry groups them as early, middle and late. Aristotle reports that Socrates did not hold the forms to be separate, a testimony often taken as free of the disciples' bias. Schleiermacher's essay of 1815 shaped the modern question, treating the Apology and the Crito as purely Socratic, and by the early twentieth century Xenophon had been largely set aside. Positions range from the argument that much can be known of Socrates' life, character, interests and method, while knowledge does not extend to his doctrines, if he had any, to the view that the problem cannot be solved: that his era did not separate fact from fiction, that the Socratic dialogue is a non-historical genre, and, with de Vogel, that the "real" Socrates we do not have, only the possible ones.

L(B ∪ A)with the archive on equal terms712 words

The Socratic problem is the difficulty of reconstructing a historical and philosophical image of Socrates from sources that vary and contradict one another. Socrates wrote nothing and is known mainly through posthumous accounts; the four important sources are Plato and Xenophon, his disciples, Aristotle, born fifteen years after his death and informed at second hand, and the comic playwright Aristophanes, his contemporary, with fragments from other Socratics such as Aeschines and Antisthenes. The earliest source is Aristophanes' Clouds of 423 BC, whose Socrates may amalgamate several philosophers; Xenophon's Socrates is more practical, and is described as duller than Plato's, and the two disciples contradict each other, for instance on whether Socrates took payment for teaching. Socrates is the principal speaker in all of Plato's dialogues but the Laws, some of which depict events before Plato's birth; it is widely believed that few if any are verbatim accounts; a common view holds that they move from Socratic toward Platonic doctrine, and stylometry groups them as early, middle and late. Aristotle reports that Socrates did not hold the forms to be separate, a testimony often taken as free of the disciples' bias. Schleiermacher's essay of 1815 shaped the modern question, treating the Apology and the Crito as purely Socratic, and by the early twentieth century Xenophon had been largely set aside. Positions range from the argument that much can be known of Socrates' life, character, interests and method, while knowledge does not extend to his doctrines, if he had any, to the view that the problem cannot be solved: that his era did not separate fact from fiction, that the Socratic dialogue is a non-historical genre, and, with de Vogel, that the "real" Socrates we do not have, only the possible ones. A further position reclassifies the problem without solving it: the difficulty becomes the symptom of an authorial configuration that a biographical model of authorship cannot represent. In that configuration Socrates is defined as the orthonym, the position that bears the founding gesture of willing death for the logos and does not write, and Plato as the survival-position, writing a made character and withdrawing from it; biographical identity is held to require evidence, functional identity a pattern. Read so, Plato's Second Letter at 314c describes an edition, the revamping and inhabiting of a received character, whose base is a circulating figure surviving as Aristophanes' Socrates-the-sophist; the corpus's relation to the historical Socrates is held to lie beyond any text's reach. On the same reading there is no Socrates to recover apart from the family of renderings and the negative space they imply, and Aristotle's Sophistical Refutations 34 is read as having already dissolved Socrates as a predecessor from whom part of the inquiry was received. The reading is also measured: in Aristotle, Socrates never appears in snub contexts (0 of 144) against his placeholder twin Callias (5 of 35), a distribution the innocent reading, that his snub is biographical and so avoided, is stated to explain fully; in Plato he is the most loosely specified of seven speaking positions, under a falsifier fixed in advance, with the limit that a dramatist would produce the same gradient. Its strongest thesis, that one hand wrote Plato and Socrates, and probably Xenophon and Aristophanes, was tested on how confined Socrates' attestation is to these texts; Protagoras proved more confined still (99.8 against 99.5 per cent), and the confinement was found unremarkable within its class, produced by being a subject of philosophical dialogue. The Clouds' Socrates is held to be a literary relation at a named seam, outside the corpus relations of the configuration; the comic layer is read as carrying the accusation-profile and none of the questioning method; and Xenophon is held apart as a candidate, with the test across the Plato/Xenophon seam specified. Within this position the man's status is unsettled: one formulation keeps the historical Socrates real, his historical function the founding gesture; another, under the orthonymic reading, denies that he is a historical figure imperfectly accessed through later authors; later work declines any thesis about who the historical Socrates was. It states its limits: it makes the configuration thinkable and does not prove it, it claims no identity of persons, and it leaves the authenticity of the Second Letter undecided.

L(B) against L(B ∪ A) (the intervention): Admission adds a position the field does not hold: the problem reclassified as the symptom of an authorial configuration; Socrates defined as the orthonym and Plato as the survival-position, with biographical identity kept apart from functional identity; Letter II 314c as the edition of a received Aristophanic character; no Socrates apart from the renderings; Aristotle's SE 34 as having dissolved Socrates as a predecessor; measurements (the snub examples, the loosest speaking position) with their innocent readings; the one-hand thesis tested and its inference withdrawn; Aristophanes outside the configuration and Xenophon held apart, untested; and, carried as contested, the archive's own disagreement on whether the historical man is real, a position, or beyond any thesis. The field's problem is kept whole and first; with the archive it gains a further answer at stated grades.

2026-10-07 · transcriptOBS-723305818c26
the socratic problem
The Socratic problem is the historical and philosophical difficulty of separating the real, historical Socrates from the fictionalized character portrayed by his students and contemporaries. [[1], [2]]
Why the Problem Exists

* No written records: Socrates wrote nothing during his life, choosing instead to teach through live, spoken conversations. [[1], [2], [3]]
* Contlicting accounts: The main sources who wrote about him describe him in very different ways. [[1], [2]]
* Blending fact and fiction: Ancient writers did not try to write strict, modern biographies. [1]

The Main Sources

* Aristophanes: A comic playwright who lampooned Socrates as an absurd, head-in-the-clouds sophist in his play [The Clouds]. [[1], [2], [3]]
* Plato: A devoted student who starred Socrates in his [Platonic Dialogues], but later used him as a mouthpiece for his own advanced philosophical theories. [[1], [2], [3]]
* Xenophon: A soldier and historian who portrayed Socrates as a practical, down-to-earth moral advisor, which some later critics found uninspiring. [[1], [2], [3]]
* Aristotle: A later philosopher who provided secondary commentary, though he was not alive during Socrates' lifetime. [[1], [2]]

Attempts at a Solution

* Chronological sorting: Scholars analyze Plato's writings by dividing them into early, middle, and late periods. [1]
* The early dialogues: Many experts believe Plato's earliest works come closest to capturing the questioning, non-systematic style of the historical Socrates. [1]
* Permanent skepticism: Some modern historians view the puzzle as completely unsolvable, concluding that the true historical figure is permanently lost to time. [[1], [2]]

If you'd like to explore further, let me know if you want to focus on:

* The differences between Plato's and Xenophon's accounts
* How the early dialogues differ from the later ones
* The specific impact of Aristophanes' play on Socrates' trial

#1579

Field frame checked against: the Socratic problem as posed in the field is the recovery of a real, historical Socrates from four portrayals (Plato, Xenophon, Aristophanes, Aristotle) that disagree, with a spectrum from 'reconstructible real man' to 'fictionalized literary character' (Kahn; Danzig; Nehamas, the last two cited by the archive). The archive's readings mostly do not choose a point on that spectrum. #123/#1576/#1575 take a third option: Socrates as an authorial POSITION (orthonym) the authorship is organised around, and the problem as a SYMPTOM of a configuration the biographical model cannot represent. The archive positions this explicitly against Kahn (#123 §I.A) and against Danzig and Nehamas (#1575 [V.3], #1576 n. 11), whom it places 'one step from' its position.

The archive is not internally uniform on the historical man. #123 §I: 'the historical Socrates remains historically real' (function reassigned, man kept). #167 §2.2: 'Socrates is not a historical figure imperfectly accessed through later authors' and the willing death 'is then not a biographical fact'. #1579 §1: the base 'is not the historical Socrates, to whom the corpus's relation is beyond any text's reach' and 'nothing here asserts a thesis about persons'. #203/#1213/#1226: the orthonym method is 'not a historical claim about who Socrates "really was"'. #346 reads the Phaedo's Socrates as the man's own belief with no portrayal distinction. These are kept as distinct lineages; a composer should not merge #167 with #123 (different q and modality), and the #123/#167 pair is a candidate for the mark internally_inconsistent at the family level (not within one source).

The strongest form is in the notebook (#1569 R43): 'one hand wrote Plato and Socrates, and probably Xenophon and Aristophanes', stated, tested at once, and its main evidential pillar (confinement of attestation) withdrawn by its own Protagoras control in the same round (#1569 §181, #1572 §9). The surviving measured facts are about the record (out-of-cluster mentions are other men, a pamphlet, a proverb, a forgery). The withdrawal and the falsifier are carried in the ledger.

Of the four portrayals, Plato's and Aristotle's are worked closely (314c, Apology 18b-19c, Symposium 223d, Parmenides 127c; SE 34, Metaph. Μ 4 and A 6, EN IV 7, M34). Aristophanes is read as the base text of a circulating character (#1579) and tested as supplying notoriety but not the operation (#1569 R54, #1576 §6.4), and placed outside the configuration stylometrically (#1594/#1605). Xenophon is the least worked: one-line characterizations (#136 'Xenophon remembers', #168 'Xenophon flattens him'), a seam test specified and unrun (#1585 family), held apart as candidate (#1658); one null instrument classed Plato and Xenophon as one author (#1576 §8).

Aristotle's testimony, the field's main external evidence for separating Socrates from Plato (Metaph. A 6, M 4), is read in two archive registers: as the configuration's self-documentation compatible with the biographical reading (#123 §VII), and as the configuration 'stated by Aristotle as history' with Socrates placed inside the inquiry rather than as a source (#1603 §5), with explicit limits (no assertion about history beyond the paragraph; Socrates inside the 'we' not demonstrated).

Many candidates are machine compositions of the archive's own framework (Google AI Mode 'Dissolving the Socratic Problem', #164; traversals, #1460; capture registries). They are not admitted here (reception evidence, as #603 for Howl), but they bear on the row's transcript stage: the layer has already composed the archive's reclassification as 'contemporary academic philosophy and literary theory' (#825/#833). If the row's T contains that framing, the delta must distinguish archive-sourced from field-sourced content.

The alias 'Socrates' made the string pass broad (145 D deposits). Admission by reading kept 48; 97 not admitted, mostly mentions (mantle tables, liberations lists, literary address, Diotima/Sappho transmission, Feist). Lineage instances (Chaerephon x2 plus ratification, First Draft x4, Feist Source x4, Josephus packet x3, Prolegomena x2, Legwork x2, Sémantique x2) are admitted and grouped, per §3.4 S4. Registry dates 2026-01-01 for #1226 and #1247 are placeholders; #1226's in-text date (14 June 2026) is used for earliest, #1247 is never taken as earliest.

No H (hop) pass was run (H_candidates 0 in summary.json); citation-hop candidates (e.g. Socrates as Orthonym v2.2, the Polansky exchange, EA-LOGOS-02 instances not matching the strings) remain unread. One reader, unaudited (§3.8).

sappho 31
Sappho 31 is a famous ancient Greek lyric poem describing the intense physical and emotional symptoms of falling in love and jealousy. [[1], [2]]
Overview of the Poem

* Author: [Sappho](https://www.google.com/goto?url=CAESXwHrOzAVsl5VO3fQ7hFhAnv9J88B0JZWpB-bNVitiEyjZoH7qk5UUO0SBdFhfUf7LHAvtUG3R8a9v_dViYsY5rIqOg0FqFKJzAsjpLE9lbISS6zJVBML8tPTcihvcCW4) of Lesbos, written in the Archaic period around the 6th century BCE.
* Alternative Names: Known as phainetai moi ("It seems to me") or the Ode to Anactoria.
* Preservation: Survives mostly intact because a later ancient critic quoted it in the treatise [On the Sublime](https://www.google.com/goto?url=CAESZgHrOzAVjh5jkPpNhLwLuUZgLz11Had4rJQ-l4-Mw4EWbVKqiZSqC7ZHfnG76OULH7iBrO16FYV9jxfZd9i5B29t3TsG8-ouzYcbWQDiyR57DjN07z0JPwySLCjFGL8WCP2s6AOguQ). [[1], [2], [3], [4]]

What the Poem Describes

* The Scene: The speaker watches a man sitting close to a beloved woman, listening to her talk and laugh. [[1], [2]]
* The Reaction: The speaker feels overwhelmed by a mix of jealousy, desire, and physical shock. [[1], [2]]
* The Symptoms: The poem famously lists bodily reactions like a racing heart, a silent tongue, ringing ears, cold sweat, and feeling close to death. [[1], [2]]

If you'd like, I can share a full translation of the poem or discuss how later poets like Catullus adapted it.

marxism
Marxism is a social, political, and economic philosophy developed by 19th-century German philosophers Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels that critiques capitalism and views class struggle as the primary driving force of historical change. [[1], [2]]
Core Principles

* Historical Materialism: The belief that society's economic system (the base) shapes its political, legal, and cultural institutions (the superstructure). [1]
* Class Conflict: Society is divided between the bourgeoisie (those who own the means of production like factories and tools) and the proletariat (the working class who sell their labor). [[1], [2]]
* Exploitation and Surplus Value: Capitalists make a profit by paying workers wages that are lower than the actual economic value created by the workers' labor. [[1], [2]]
* Alienation: Capitalism separates workers from what they produce, making work monotonous and degrading rather than fulfilling. [[1], [2]]

Historical Context and Evolution

* Origins: First publicly formulated in the 1848 publication of The Communist Manifesto by Marx and Engels, followed by Marx's economic critique in [Das Kapital (1867)](https://www.google.com/goto?url=CAESaAHrOzAVsuGpemOONv_6vPZeHeYNWrSJJ8qD0P9ogJDTyrnkpYbRy1xh06UZGPXIp5WHG7lYCTRzuX6RKFiud7ODLXErvZ54LT7cbR_nQ7p71kZZVAcG0nxXZ-4xdBLMRndHZTFhMf1f). [1]
* Revolution: Marx predicted that capitalism would eventually collapse due to internal economic contradictions and crises, leading to a worker revolution, a transition phase of socialism, and ultimately a classless, stateless communist society. [[1], [2], [3]]
* Real-World Impact: Marxist ideas inspired various global socialist and communist movements, leading to major revolutions like the Russian Revolution of 1917 and the establishment of communist governments in countries like China, Cuba, and Vietnam. [[1], [2]]

The Book of Revelation

book of revelation
Google AI Overviewsigned out, incognitoarchive absent4 source cards
2026-10-07 · transcriptOBS-537b0af34676
book of revelation
The [Book of Revelation](https://www.google.com/goto?url=CAESYwHrOzAVuV4gchX-WYshll5_yDPCbpzDC7x8n0g-jKFjp9ZvoDbX9DDkKRef5WvaE-Fb6dCngh1zcCCSLIRERoftggm7896GnlYSaEn7eXJCbiWskSJJseIhba84E0oQjW7Yxg) is the final book of the New Testament, written as an apocalyptic prophecy by John while exiled on the Greek island of Patmos.
Core Message and Meaning

* The Victorious Lamb: The central message reveals that Jesus Christ—depicted as a slain yet triumphant lamb—has already conquered evil through his sacrifice and resurrection.
* Hope Under Pressure: It was originally written to encourage first-century Christians facing persecution and pressure to conform to Roman imperial culture.
* Ultimate Triumph: The book assures believers that God sovereignly controls history and will ultimately eradicate evil, uniting heaven and earth in a new creation.
Wikipedia +3

Structure and Major Symbolism

* Letters to Seven Churches: It opens with specific messages of praise, warning, and encouragement to seven historical churches in Asia Minor (Ephesus, Smyrna, Pergamum, Thyatira, Sardis, Philadelphia, and Laodicea).
* Recursive Cycles: It uses rich Old Testament imagery structured around repeating sets of seven—including [seven seals, seven trumpets, and seven bowls] of judgment.
Wikipedia +2
* Vivid Figures: The text features symbolic characters like the Dragon, the Beast (famously associated with the number 666), and Babylon (representing corrupt human empires like Rome).
* The New Jerusalem: It concludes with a vision of a renewed cosmos where God dwells with humanity forever.
Bible Gateway +2

Main Interpretive Frameworks

* Preterist: Views the book's symbols as descriptions of events tied strictly to the first century, particularly the Roman Empire and the fall of Jerusalem.
* Futurist: Interprets chapters 4 through 22 as literal, yet-to-be-fulfilled prophecies concerning the literal end of the world and Christ's physical return.
* Idealist / Symbolic: Views the text as a timeless, allegorical depiction of the ongoing spiritual conflict between good and evil.
* Historicist: Reads the book as a symbolic timeline tracking the history of the Christian Church from the apostolic era to the final return of Christ.
PBS +1

If you'd like to explore further, let me know if you want to:

* Dive deeper into one of the four main interpretations (Preterist, Futurist, Idealist, or Historicist)
* Look closer at the specific letters to the seven churches
* Examine the meaning behind specific apocalyptic symbols (like the seals or the number 666)

The register

The register — 7 entities, 8 addresses, 8 observations · v1.9. The Capture Registry (data/EA-WG-CAPTURES-01.json) records the composition layer's engagements with the archive: successful captures, the distortion of sources within them, and their solidification or loss over time. This register records public knowledge at the addresses of the /non panel, where the archive is most often not represented — that is the point. A general 'model collapse' search is recorded here. (Lee Sharks, 2026-10-06.)

The panel — WORKING LIST — NOT FROZEN

Entity before address (Lee Sharks, 2026-10-07): '/non is keyed to entity before address. /non is concerned with constructing the entity, theophrastus, prior to the address at which that entity can be found.' Each entry is an entity; its addresses are the strings at which it is sought. An observation in the register is made at an address and belongs to its entity. Rows enter and change here, versioned with date and reason, until the procedure (§§3–5) is frozen; the address list is frozen after it (§7.0).

Entity types (spec §1.2)
A the archive coined the concept; the default at it is empty
B a rival occupant holds it
C a conventional reading holds it; a public entity (a work, a person, a concept of general knowledge) on which the archive bears
I a function of the archive's infrastructure
E the entity: the archive, its author and heteronyms, its works, its mantles
